   1. Charles Babbage is considered as the “father of the computer” by some people. That is because he
      pd,aia nefíÊj “mß.Klfha mshd” f,ig iuyre y÷kaj;s' thg fya;=j ù we;af;a Tyq"

          1) invented the mechanical calculator Pascaline.
             meial,ska (Pascaline) hdka;%sl .Kl hka;%h ks¾udKh l< neúks'
          2) invented the first re-programmable electronic computing machine.
             kej; l%uf,aLkh l< yels m<uq bf,lafg%dksl wd.Kk hka;%h ks¾udKh l< neúks'
          3) took the leadership in building the first personal computer at IBM.
             IBM iud.fï § m<uq mqoa., mß.Klh ksmoùu i|yd kdhl;ajh ,nd ÿka neúks'
          4) introduced the concept of “Input, Process and Output” that is used in modern computers, for
             the first time.
             kùk mß.Klj, Ndú; jk “wdodkh" l%shdj,sh yd m%;sodkh” hk ixl,amh m<uqjrg y÷kajd
             ÿka neúks'
          5) is the founder of the first electronic digital computer ENIAC (Electronic Numerical Integrator
             And Computer).
             m<uq bf,lafg%dksl ixLHdxl mß.Klh ENIAC (Electronic Numerical Integrator And
             Computer) ys uQ,drïNlhd jQ neúks'

          Answer: 4)

          Explanation / meyeÈ,s lsÍu

          Option/ úl,amh 1)
          The Pascaline, one of the earliest mechanical calculators, was invented by Blaise Pascal, a French
          mathematician, in the 17th century. It was capable of performing basic addition and subtraction. Charles
          Babbage, although a pioneer in computing, had no involvement in the invention of the Pascaline. He
          worked on more complex mechanical computing machines like the Difference Engine and the Analytical
          Engine.
          merKs;u hdka;%sl .Kl hka;%j,ska tlla jk meial,hska" 17 jk ishjfia§ m%xY .Ks;{fhl= jk
          íf,aia meial,a úiska fidhd .kakd ,§' th uQ,sl tl;= lsÍu iy wvq lsÍu isÿ lsÍug iu;a úh'
          mß.KllrKfha mqfrda.dñfhl= jqjo pd,aia nefíÊ meial,hska fidhd .ekSug iïnkaO fkdùh' Tyq
          ä*rkaia tkaðu iy úYaf,aIK tkaðu jeks jvd;a ixlS¾K hdka;%sl mß.Kl hka;% u; jev lf<ah'

          Option/ úl,amh 2)
          The first re-programmable electronic computer is widely recognized as the ENIAC or, later, the EDVAC,
          which introduced the stored-program concept. These machines were developed long after Babbage’s time.
          Charles Babbage's Analytical Engine, while conceptually programmable using punched cards, was never
          completed and was entirely mechanical, not electronic. Therefore, he cannot be credited with inventing a
          reprogrammable electronic computing machine.
          m<uq kej; jevigyka.; l< yels bf,lafg%dksl mß.Klh ENIAC fyda miqj .nvd l< jevigyka
          ixl,amh y÷kajd ÿka EDVAC f,i mq¿,a f,i ms<s.efka' fuu hka;% nefíÊf.a ld,fhka fndfyda
          l,lg miqj ixj¾Okh lrk ,§' pd,aia nefíÊf.a úYaf,aIKd;aul tkaðu" isÿrem;a Ndú;fhka
          ixl,amuh jYfhka jevigyka.; l< yels jqjo" lsis úfgl;a iïmQ¾K fkdjQ w;r th iïmQ¾Kfhkau
          hdka;%sl úh - bf,lafg%dksl fkdfõ' tneúka" kej; jevigyka.; l< yels bf,lafg%dksl mß.Kl
          hka;%hla fidhd .ekSfï f.!rjh Tyqg ysñ fkdfõ'

    Option/ úl,amh 3)
    Charles Babbage lived in the 19th century and died in 1871, long before IBM or personal computers
    existed. The first personal computers were developed in the 1970s and 1980s, with IBM introducing its
    version in 1981. Therefore, Babbage could not have played any role in this development. This statement
    is anachronistic and historically inaccurate.
    pd,aia nefíÊ 19 jk ishjfia Ôj;a jQ w;r IBM fyda mqoa.,sl mß.Kl mej;Sug fndfyda l,lg fmr

    Option/ úl,amh 4)
    While Charles Babbage did not explicitly name the model as "Input, Process, Output", his design of the
    Analytical Engine included these fundamental components. The machine had an input method a
    processing unit, and an output mechanism. These ideas formed the basis of the architecture of modern
    computers, though the IPO model as a formal concept came much later.
    pd,aia nefíÊ úiska wdlD;sh wdodkh" l%shdj,sh" m%;sodkh f,i meyeÈ,sj kï fkdl<o" Tyqf.a
    úYaf,aIK tkaðfï ie,iqug fuu uQ,sl ixrpl we;=<;a úh' hka;%hg wdodk l%uhla ieliqï tAllhla
    iy m%;sodk hdka;%Khla ;snKs' fuu woyia kùk mß.Klj, wdlD;sfha moku ieliQ kuq;a úêu;a
    ixl,amhla f,i IPO wdlD;sh fndfyda l,lg miqj meñKsfhah'
    Option/ úl,amh 5)
    ENIAC was developed in the United States by John Presper Eckert and John Mauchly in the 1940s. It was
    the first general-purpose electronic digital computer, but it came about long after Charles Babbage's time.
    While Babbage laid the theoretical foundation for digital computing with his designs, he was not involved
    with ENIAC or any electronic computer, as electronics were not yet developed during his era.
    ENIAC 1940 .Kkaj, fcdaka fm%iam¾ tl¾Ü iy fcdaka ujqÉ,s úiska tlai;a ckmofha ixj¾Okh lrk
    ,§' th m<uq fmdÿ ld¾h bf,lafg%dksl äðg,a mß.Klh jQ kuq;a th pd,aia nefíÊf.a ld,fhka

    Tyqf.a hq.fha bf,lafg%dksl WmlrK ;ju;a ixj¾Okh lr fkd;snqKs'

    Therefore, option 4) is the correct answer.

 2. The first generation computers were based on
    m<uqjk mrïmrdfõ mß.Kl i|yd mdol jQfha"

    1)   Very Large Scale Integration (VLSI) technology / b;d úYd, mßudKfha wkql,s; ;dlaIKh fõ'
    2)   Large Scale Integration (LSI) technology / úYd, mßudKfha wkql,s; ;dlaIKh fõ'
    3)   Integrated Circuits (ICs) / wkql,s; mßm: fõ'
    4)   Transistors / g%dkaisiagr fõ'
    5)   Vacuum tubes / ßla; k< fõ'

    Answer: 5)
    Explanation / meyeÈ,s lsÍu
    Option/ úl,amh 1)
    Very Large-Scale Integration (VLSI) technology involves placing thousands to millions of transistors
    onto a single chip. This technology gained widespread use in the fourth generation of computers, beginning
    around the 1980s. VLSI made it possible to create microprocessors, which drastically improved
    computing speed, efficiency, and size. This is much more advanced than the technology used in the first-
    generation computers. Therefore, VLSI is not related to the first generation at all.
    b;d úYd, mßudK tAldnoaO lsÍfï VLSI ;dlaIKh uÕska g%dkaisiagr oyia .Kkska iy ñ,shk .Kkska
    ;ks Ñmhla njg m;a lrk ,§' fuu ;dlaIKh 1980 .Kkaj, muK isg isõjk mrïmrdfõ
    mß.Klj, mq¿,a Ndú;hla ,nd .;af;ah' VLSI uÕska laIqø ilikhka ks¾udKh lsÍug yels jQ w;r
    tuÕska mß.Kl fõ.h" ld¾hlaIu;dj iy m%udKh fnfyúka jeä ÈhqKq úh' fuh m<uq mrïmrdfõ
    mß.Klj, Ndú;d lrk ,o ;dlaIKh jvd fnfyúka ÈhqKq h' tneúka" VLSI lsisfia;au m<uq
    mrïmrdjg iïnkaO fkdfõ'
RAVINDU BANDARANAYAKE                               -3-                                          #ictfromabc


    Option/ úl,amh 2)
    Large Scale Integration (LSI) technology allowed for hundreds to thousands of transistors to be integrated
    on a chip. LSI became prominent during the third generation of computers in the 1970s, just before VLSI.
    Like VLSI, this technology also significantly improved performance and reduced the size of computers.
    However, LSI did not exist during the time of first-generation computers, which makes this option
    historically inaccurate.
    úYd, mßudK tAldnoaO lsÍfï LSI ;dlaIKh Ñmhla u; g%dkaisiagr ish .Kkla isg oyia .Kkla
    tAldnoaO lsÍug bv ie,iSh' 1970 .Kkaj, ;=kajk mrïmrdfõ mß.Kl w;r;=r" VLSI g fmr LSI
    m%uqL úh' VLSI fuka" fuu ;dlaIKh o ld¾h idOkh ie,lsh hq;= f,i jeäÈhqKq l< w;r
    mß.Klj, m%udKh wvq lf<ah' flfia fj;;a" m<uq mrïmrdfõ mß.Kl mej;s ld,h ;=< LSI
    fkd;snQ w;r" tuÕska fuu úl,amh ft;sydislj idjoH fõ'

    Option/ úl,amh 3)
    Integrated Circuits (ICs) are a major characteristic of third-generation computers. ICs were developed in
    the 1960s, allowing multiple transistors and electronic components to be embedded on a single chip. First-
    generation computers came much earlier, in the late 1940s to mid-1950s, when ICs had not yet been
    invented. Thus, while ICs revolutionized computing, they are not part of first-generation technology.
    tAldnoaO mßm: (IC) hkq ;=kajk mrïmrdfõ mß.Klj, m%Odk ,laIKhls' 1960 .Kkaj, IC
    ixj¾Okh lrk ,o w;r" tuÕska nyq g%dkaisiagr iy bf,lafg%dksl Wmdx. ;ks Ñmhla u; ;ekam;a
    lsÍug bv ie,iKs' m<uq mrïmrdfõ mß.Kl fndfyda l,lg fmr meñKsfhah" 1940 .Kkaj,
    w.Nd.fha isg 1950 .Kkaj, ueo Nd.h olajd" tA jk úg IC fidhd f.k fkd;snqKs' fï wkqj" IC
    mß.KllrKfha úma,ùh fjkila isÿ l<o" tAjd m<uq mrïmrdfõ ;dlaIKfha fldgila fkdfõ'

    Option/ úl,amh 4)
    Transistors replaced vacuum tubes and were the core technology in second-generation computers (mid-
    1950s to early 1960s). They were smaller, more energy-efficient, and more reliable than vacuum tubes.
    While a big leap forward in computing technology, transistors were not used in the first-generation
    computers. Hence, this option is not correct for the first generation, although it applies to the second.
    g%dkaisiagr ßla; k, m%;sia:dmkh l< w;r fojk mrïmrdfõ mß.Klj, ^1950 oYlfha ueo Nd.fha
    isg 1960 oYlfha uq,a Nd.h olajd& uQ,sl ;dlaIKh úh' tAjd ßla; k, j,g jvd l=vd" n,Yla;s
    ld¾hlaIu iy úYajdiodhl úh' mß.Kl ;dlaIKfha úYd, bÈß msïula jqjo" m<uq mrïmrdfõ
    mß.Klj, g%dkaisiagr Ndú;d fkdlf<ah' tneúka" fuu úl,amh m<uq mrïmrdjg ksjerÈ fkdfõ"
    kuq;a th fojk mrïmrdjg wod< fõ'

    Option/ úl,amh 5)
    First-generation computers (approximately 1940s–1956) were based on vacuum tube technology. Vacuum
    tubes were used for circuitry and magnetic drums for memory. These computers were massive, consumed
    a lot of electricity, and generated significant heat. Examples include ENIAC, UNIVAC, and IBM 701.
    Despite their limitations, vacuum tubes were the core technology of this era, making this the correct
    answer.
    m<uq mrïmrdfõ mß.Kl ^wdikak jYfhka 1940 - 1956& ßla; k, ;dlaIKh u; mokï úh' u;lh
    i|yd mßm: iy pqïnl fnr i|yd ßla; k, Ndú;d lrk ,§' fuu mß.Kl oejeka; úh" úÿ,sh
    úYd, m%udKhla mßfNdackh l< w;r ie,lsh hq;= ;dmhla ckkh lf<ah' WodyrK f,i ENIAC
    UNIVAC iy IBM 701 we;=<;a fõ' tAjdfha iSudjka ;sìh§;a" ßla; k, fuu hq.fha uQ,sl ;dlaIKh
    jQ w;r" fuh ksjerÈ ms<s;=r njg m;a lf<ah'

    Therefore, option 5) is the correct answer.
    tneúka" 5) úl,amh ksjerÈ ms<s;=r fõ'

 3. The decimal number equivalent to 1101102 is
    1101102 i|yd ;=,H jk oYuh ixLHdj

    1) 39               2) 48               3) 54             4) 55             5) 108

    Answer: 3)

    Explanation / meyeÈ,s lsÍu

    Let’s do the calculation as follows.
    .Kkh lsÍu my; mßÈ lruq'

     1 1 0 1 1 0
     25 24 23 22 21 20
     32 16 0 4 2 0
          32+16+4+2
              54

    Therefore, option 3) is the correct answer.
    tneúka" 3) úl,amh ksjerÈ ms<s;=r fõ'

 4. Consider the following list rendered by a web browser:
    fjí w;ßlaiqjla u.ska úoeyq (render) lrk ,o my; olajd we;s ,ehsia;=j i,lkak:

    1. Pineapple
    2. Mango
    3. Banana

    Which of the following HTML tags can be used to create the above list?
    by; ,ehsia;=j ks¾udKh lsÍu i|yd my; i|yka l=uk HTML Wiq,k (tags) Ndú; l< yels o?

    1) <dd>                  2) <dl>                3) <li>           4) <ol>        5) <ul>

    Answer: 4)

    Let's first learn about the types of lists in HTML and the tags used in their layout.
    wm uq,skau HTML yS we;s ,ehsia;= j¾. yd tAjdyS§ fhdod.kakd ge.a ms<snoj oek .ksuq'
    • <ol> → Ordered List (uses numbers: 1, 2, 3...)
    • <ul> → Unordered List (uses bullets: •)
    • <li> → List Item (used inside <ol> or <ul>, not alone)
    • <dl> → Description List
    • <dd> → Description term details (used with <dl>)

    Here is an Ordered List and the code for this is shown below.
    fuys we;af;a Ordered List tlla jk w;r fuhg wod, fla;h my;ska oelafõ'

    <ol>
      <li>Pineapple</li>
      <li>Mango</li>
      <li>Banana</li>
    </ol>

    Therefore, option 4) is the correct answer.
    tneúka" 4) úl,amh ksjerÈ ms<s;=r fõ'

 5. Random Access Memory (RAM) modules are often compared by their capacity, measured in
    ...................... and by their speed, measured in ...................... .
    iiïNdú m%fõY u;l (RAM) fudähq, ksrka;rfhka ixikaokh lrkq ,nkafka ..........................
    u.ska ukskq ,nk tajdfha Odß;dj iy .......................... u.ska ukskq ,nk fõ.h mokï lrf.k
    h'
    Most suitable words to fill the blanks of the above statements are respectively
    by; j.ka;sfha ysia;eka msrùu i|yd jvd;a fhda.H joka jkafka ms<sfj<ska"
    1)   Kilobytes, Gigabytes / lsf,danhsÜia" .s.dnhsÜia
    2)   Gigabytes, Megabits per second / .s.dnhsÜia" ;;amrhg fu.dìÜia
    3)   Gigabytes, Megahertz / .s.dnhsÜia" fu.dy¾Üia
    4)   Megahertz, Kilohertz / fu.dy¾Üia" lsf,day¾Üia
    5)   Gigabits, Megabits per second / .s.dìÜia" ;;amrhg fu.dìÜi
    Answer: 3)
    Explanation / meyeÈ,s lsÍu
    Option/ úl,amh 1)
    Kilobytes (KB) are a very small unit of digital storage, typically used in the early days of computing when
    memory was limited. Modern RAM is no longer measured in kilobytes — even the smallest modules today
    are in gigabytes (GB). Also, gigabytes are not a unit of speed, but of capacity. So, both the order and the
    choice of units in this option are incorrect, making this a misleading choice.
    lsf,danhsÜ ^KB& hkq äðg,a .nvd lsÍfï b;d l=vd tAllhla jk w;r" idudkHfhka u;lh iSñ; jQ
    mß.KllrKfha uq,a Èkj, Ndú;d lrk ,§' kùk RAM ;jÿrg;a lsf,danhsÜ j,ska ukskq fkd,efí
    - wo l=vdu fudähq, mjd .s.dnhsÜ (GB) j,ska ukskq ,efí' tfiau" .s.dnhsÜ hkq fõ.fha tAllhla
    fkdj Odß;djfha tAllhls' tneúka" fuu úl,amfha tAll wkqms<sfj, iy f;aÍu hk folu jerÈ
    neúka fuh fkduÕ hjk f;aÍula lrhs'
    Option/ úl,amh 2)
    RAM capacity is indeed measured in gigabytes (GB). As for speed, "megabits per second (Mbps)" is
    commonly used in networking and data transfer rates, not typically for RAM speed. RAM speed is usually
    specified in megahertz (MHz) or data rate (e.g., DDR4-3200). So, while the speed unit here is technically
    a rate, it is not the standard way to express RAM speed.
    RAM Odß;dj we;a; jYfhkau .s.dnhsÜ (GB) j,ska ukskq ,efí' fõ.h iïnkaOfhka .;a l,"
    ;;amrhg fu.dìÜ (Mbps) cd,lrKfha iy o;a; yqjudre wkqmd;j, nyq,j Ndú;d fõ" idudkHfhka
    RAM fõ.h i|yd fkdfõ' RAM fõ.h idudkHfhka fu.dy¾Üia (MHz) fyda o;a; wkqmd;fhka ^Wod(
    DDR4-3200& olajd we;' tneúka" fuys fõ. tAllh ;dlaIKsl jYfhka wkqmd;hla jqjo" RAM fõ.h
    m%ldY lsÍug iïu; l%uh th fkdfõ'
    Option/ úl,amh 3)
    This is the most accurate answer. RAM capacity is measured in gigabytes (GB), indicating how much data
    it can hold at once. RAM speed is commonly expressed in megahertz (MHz), which refers to the frequency
    of operations — essentially, how many cycles per second it can handle. A higher MHz typically means
    faster data access and better performance (though other factors like latency also matter). This is the
    standard format used in technical specifications.
    fuh jvd;a ksjerÈ ms<s;=rhs' RAM Odß;dj .s.dnhsÜ (GB) j,ska ukskq ,nk w;r" th tljr fldmuK
    o;a; m%udKhla r|jd .; yelso hkak olajhs' RAM fõ.h idudkHfhka fu.dy¾Üia (MHz) j,ska
    m%ldY jk w;r th fufyhqï ixLHd;h - uQ,sl jYfhka ;;amrhlg fldmuK pl% m%udKhla yeisrúh
    yelso hkak olajhs' by< MHz hkq idudkHfhka fõ.j;a o;a; m%fõYhla iy jvd fyd| ld¾h
    idOkhla woyia lrhs ^m%udoh jeks wfkl=;a idOl o jeo.;a fõ&' ;dlaIKsl msßú;rhkays Ndú;d
    jk iïu; wdlD;sh fuhhs'
    Option/ úl,amh 4)
    Megahertz (MHz) and kilohertz (kHz) are units of frequency, not capacity. Capacity should be measured
    in bytes (like GB or MB), not in hertz. Using MHz to refer to capacity and kHz to refer to speed is a
    fundamental misunderstanding of RAM metrics. This option is entirely incorrect for this context.
    Megahertz (MHz) iy kilohertz (kHz) hkq ixLHd;fha tAll ñi Odß;dj fkdfõ' Odß;dj ueksh hq;af;a
    y¾Üia j,ska fkdj nhsÜ j,ska ^GB fyda MB jeks&' Odß;dj i|yka lsÍug MHz iy fõ.h i|yka
    lsÍug kHz Ndú;d lsÍu RAM ñkqï ms<sn| uQ,sl jerÈ jegySuls' fuu ikao¾Nh i|yd fuu úl,amh
    iïmQ¾Kfhkau jerÈhs'
RAVINDU BANDARANAYAKE                              -6-                                        #ictfromabc


    Option/ úl,amh 5)
    Gigabits (Gb) and Megabits per second (Mbps) are more commonly used in network hardware or SSD
    storage interface speeds, not for RAM modules. RAM is typically measured in gigabytes (GB), not
    gigabits (Gb) — even though 1 byte = 8 bits. Similarly, Mbps is again more relevant for data transfer
    speeds across networks rather than RAM clock speed. So, while technically coherent, it's not standard
    usage for RAM specs.
    .s.dìÜ (Gb) iy ;;amrhg fu.dìÜ (Mbps) RAM fudähq, i|yd fkdj cd, oDvdx. fyda SSD .nvd
    w;=reuqyqK;a fõ.j, nyq,j Ndú;d fõ' RAM idudkHfhka ukskq ,nkafka .s.dìÜ (Gb) j,ska fkdj
    .s.dìÜ (GB) j,ska - 1 nhsÜ = ìgq 8 la jqjo' tA yd iudkj" RAM Trf,daiq fõ.hg jvd cd, yryd
    o;a; yqjudre fõ.h i|yd Mbp kej;;a jvd;a wod< fõ' tneúka" ;dlaIKslj iqixfhda.S jqjo" th
    RAM msßú;r i|yd iïu; Ndú;h fkdfõ'
    Therefore, option 3) is the correct answer.
    tneúka" 3) úl,amh ksjerÈ ms<s;=r fõ'

 6. An application which requires more memory space than the maximum memory space available in
    the primary memory of a computer is ready for execution. Which of the followings is used by the
    operating system of that computer to satisfy this need?
    mß.Klhl m%d:ñl u;lfha Wmßu u;l wjldYhg jvd jeä wjldYhla wjYH jk fhÿula
    Odjkh i|yd iQodkï ù we;' tu mß.Klfha fufyhqï moaO;sh my; i|yka l=ula Wmfhda.s
    lrf.k fuu wjYH;dj imqrd,hs o?
    1)   Random Access Memory / iiïNdú m%fõY u;lh (RAM)
    2)   Read Only Memory / mGk ud;% u;lh (ROM)
    3)   Cache Memory / ksys; u;lh
    4)   Virtual Memory / w;:HrEms u;lh
    5)   Extended Memory / úia;D; u;lh
    Answer: 4)
    Explanation / meyeÈ,s lsÍu
    Option/ úl,amh 1)
    RAM is the primary memory of a computer, used to store data and instructions while programs are
    running. However, in this question, the problem is that RAM is already full or insufficient for the
    application. Therefore, the operating system cannot solve this problem by using more RAM.
    RAM hkq mß.Klhl m%d:ñl u;lh jk w;r th jevigyka l%shd;aul jk w;r;=r o;a; iy
    Wmfoia .nvd lsÍug Ndú;d lrhs' flfia fj;;a" fuu m%Yakfha§" .eg¿j jkafka RAM oekgu;a
    fhÿu i|yd msÍ ;sîu fyda m%udKj;a fkdùuhs' tneúka" fufyhqï moaO;shg jeä RAM m%udKhla
    Ndú;d lsÍfuka fuu .eg¿j úi¢h fkdyel'
    Option/ úl,amh 2)
    ROM is non-volatile memory that contains firmware or permanent instructions, such as the computer’s
    BIOS or bootloader. It is not used for running applications or handling memory overflows. ROM is not
    writable during normal operation, and its content doesn’t change, so it’s irrelevant to the problem of
    memory overflow in applications. Thus, this is not a suitable answer.
    ROM hkq iaÓrdx. fyda mß.Klfha BIOS fyda bootloader jeks iaÓrdx. fyda iaÓr Wmfoia wvx.= kIH
    fkdjk u;lhls' th fhÿï Odjkh lsÍug fyda u;l msgdr .e,Sï yeisrùug Ndú;d fkdlrhs' idudkH
    l%shdldß;ajh w;r;=r ROM ,súh fkdyels w;r tys wka;¾.;h fjkia fkdfõ" tneúka th fhÿïj,
    u;l msgdr .e,Sfï .eg¨jg wod< fkdfõ' fï wkqj" fuh iqÿiq ms<s;=rla fkdfõ'
    Option/ úl,amh 3)
    Cache memory is a small, very fast memory located close to the CPU. It temporarily stores frequently
    accessed instructions and data to speed up processing. However, cache memory is extremely limited in
    size and is not used by the OS to handle memory shortages. It does not compensate for a lack of primary
    memory; instead, it complements it by improving speed.
    ksys; u;lh hkq CPU wi, msysgd we;s l=vd" b;d fõ.j;a u;lhls' th ieliqï fõ.j;a lsÍu i|yd
    ks;r m%fõY jk Wmfoia iy o;a; ;djld,slj .nvd lrhs' flfia fj;;a" ksys; u;lh m%udKfhka
    w;sYhska iSñ; jk w;r u;l ysÕhka yeisrùug OS úiska Ndú;d fkdflf¾' th m%d:ñl u;lfha
    W!k;djhg jkaÈ ,nd fkdfoa. tA fjkqjg" th fõ.h jeä ÈhqKq lsÍfuka th iïmQ¾K lrhs'
RAVINDU BANDARANAYAKE                               -7-                                           #ictfromabc


    Option/ úl,amh 4)
    Virtual memory is a memory management technique used by operating systems when RAM is
    insufficient to hold all the active programs and data. It works by temporarily transferring data from
    RAM to a section of the hard drive or SSD (often called a "swap file" or "page file"). This allows larger
    applications to run than what the physical RAM could support alone. So when the application needs more
    space than available RAM, virtual memory is the solution the OS uses.
    w;:HrEmS u;lh hkq ish¨u l%shdldÍ jevigyka iy o;a; r|jd .ekSug RAM m%udKj;a fkdjk
    úg fufyhqï moaO;s úiska Ndú;d lrk u;l l<ukdlrK ;dlaIKhls' th RAM fj;ska oDV ;eáfha
    fyda SSD fldgilg ^fndfyda úg swap f.dkqj fyda msgq f.dkqj f,i ye¢kafõ& ;djld,slj o;a; udre
    lsÍfuka l%shd lrhs' fuh fN!;sl RAM tlg muKla iydh úh yels m%udKhg jvd úYd, fhÿï
    l%shd;aul lsÍug bv i,ihs' tneúka fhÿug mj;sk RAM j,g jvd jeä bvla wjYH jQ úg" w:;H
    u;lh hkq OS Ndú;d lrk úi÷uhs'
    Option/ úl,amh 5)
    Extended memory refers to memory beyond the first 1MB in older DOS-based systems. It was relevant
    during the time of early Intel x86 processors (like the 80286 and 80386). In modern systems, the concept
    of "extended memory" is obsolete and has been replaced by more advanced memory addressing and
    virtual memory management. So while this term had meaning in the past, it is not the correct solution
    for modern systems dealing with memory shortages.
    úia;D; u;lh hkq merKs DOS-mdol moaO;sj, m<uq 1MB blaujd u;lhhs' th uq,a Intel x86
    ilikhka ^80286 iy 80386 jeks& ld,h ;=< wod< úh' kùk moaO;sj," È.= l< u;lh hk
    ixl,amh h,a mek f.dia we;s w;r th jvd;a ÈhqKq u;l ,smsk iy w:;H u;l l<ukdlrKh
    u.ska m%;sia:dmkh lr we;' tneúka fuu fhÿug w;S;fha w¾:hla ;snqKo" u;l ysÕhka iuÕ lghq;=
    lrk kùk moaO;s i|yd th ksjerÈ úi÷u fkdfõ'
    Therefore, option 4) is the correct answer.

 9. Which of the following is not a typical use of the Random Access Memory (RAM) of a personal
    computer?
    mqoa., mß.Klhl (Personal Computer) we;s iiïNdú m%fõY u;lfhys (RAM) o¾YSh Ndú;hla
    (typical use) fkdjkafka my; i|yka ljrla o?

    3)   Providing storage for operating system / fufyhqï moaO;sh i|yd wdphkh (storage) iemiSu

    5)   Keeping the BIOS program for boot-up / m%fõIKh lsÍu i|yd BIOS l%uf,aLh mj;ajd .ekSu

    Explanation / meyeÈ,s lsÍu
    Option/ úl,amh 1)
    This is a correct use of RAM. When a computer runs programs, it temporarily stores the data being actively
    used or processed in RAM for quick access by the CPU.
    fuh RAM ksjerÈj Ndú;d lsÍuls' mß.Klhla jevigyka l%shd;aul lrk úg" th l%shdldÍj Ndú;d

    ilikh l%shd;aul jk w;r;=r tAjd ld¾hlaIuj l%shd;aul l< yelsh'

    sending to a printer), making the process smoother and faster.

 11. Consider the following combinatory circuit implemented using universal gates:
     my; fmkajd we;s id¾j;% (universal) oajdr wdOdrfhka ia:dms; lrk ,o ixhqla; (combinatory)
     mßm:h i,lkak(



    Input                                Output




    The above circuit is equivalent to a/an
    by; mßm:h iu;=,H jkqfha"

    1) AND Gate         2) OR Gate          3) NAND Gate 4) NOR Gate               5) NOT Gate

    Answer: 2)

    This logic circuit, made up of universal gates (NAND), is equivalent to a basic logic gate used in digital
    logic design.
    id¾j;% oajdr (NAND) j,ska iukaú; fuu ;d¾lsl mßm:h" äðg,a ;d¾lsl ks¾udKfha Ndú;d jk
    uQ,sl ;d¾lsl oajdrhlg iudk fõ'

    Let’s build up the logical expression for the given logic circuit assuming the inputs are A and B.
    wdodk A iy B hehs Wml,amkh lrñka § we;s ;d¾lsl mßm:h i|yd ;d¾lsl m%ldYkh f.dvk.uq'

    ̅̅̅̅̅̅̅̅̅̅̅̅
    ̅̅̅̅̅
    A. A . ̅̅̅̅̅
           B. B
    Let’s simplify the given expression using Boolean laws.
    nQ,Sh kS;s Ndú;fhka § we;s m%ldYkh ir, lruq'

    ̅̅̅̅̅̅̅̅̅̅̅̅
    ̅̅̅̅̅
    A. A . ̅̅̅̅̅
           B. B
    ̿̿̿̿̿
    A. A + ̿̿̿̿̿
             B. B   (De Morgan’s law / ã fuda.kaf.a kHdh)
    A. A + B. B     (Double complement law / oaú;aj m%;sf,dau kHdh)
    A+B             (Idempotent law / ;foajNdú kHdh)

    The simplification shows that the given logic circuit is equivalent to an OR gate.
    ir, lsÍfuka fmkakqï lrkafka § we;s ;d¾lsl mßm:h OR oajdrhlg iudk njhs'

    Therefore, option 2) is the correct answer.
    tneúka" 2) úl,amh ksjerÈ ms<s;=r fõ'

 12. ............................ is used for analog signal to digital signal conversion.
     m%;siu ix{djla ixLHdxl ix{djla njg mßj¾;kh lsÍug ....................... Ndú; lrkq ,efí'
    Which of the following is most appropriate to fill the blank in the above statement?
    by; j.ka;sfha ysia;ek msrùug jvd;a u fhda.H jkafka my; i|yka ljrla o?
    1)   Amplitude Modulation / úia;dr uQ¾ckh (AM)
    2)   Frequency Modulation / ixLHd; uQ¾ckh (FM)
    3)   Pulse Code Modulation / iamkaÈ; fla; uQ¾ckh (PCM)
    4)   Phase Modulation / l,d uQ¾ckh (PM)
    5)   Time Division Modulation / ld, fnÿï uQ¾ckh (AM)

    Answer: 3)

    Modulation is the process of varying one or more properties (such as its amplitude, frequency, or phase)
    of periodic waveform called the carrier signal to encode information for transmission over a
    communication channel.
    uqyq¾cKh hkq ikaksfõok kd,sldjla yryd iïfm%aIKh i|yd f;dr;=re fla;kh lsÍug jdyl ix{dj
    f,iska ye¢kafjk wdj¾;sl ;rx. wdlD;shl .=Kdx. tlla fyda lsysmhla ^tys úia;drh" ixLHd;h
    fyda l,dj jeks& fjkia lsÍfï l%shdj,shhs'

    Amplitude Modulation (AM) / úia;dr uQ¾ckh
    In amplitude modulation, the amplitude of the carrier signal is varied in proportion to the amplitude of the
    information (baseband) signal, while the frequency and phase of the carrier remain constant.
    úia;drh uqyq¾ckfha§" jdyl ix{dfõ úia;drh f;dr;=re ix{dfõ úia;drhg iudkqmd;slj fjkia
    lrk w;r" jdylfha ixLHd;h iy l,dj ksh;j mj;S'

    In AM, the louder the signal (for example, a voice or music), the higher the amplitude of the carrier wave,
    and the quieter the signal, the lower the amplitude.
    AM j,§" ix{dj ^WodyrKhla f,i" yvla fyda ix.S;hla& jeä jk ;rug" jdyl ;rx.fha úia;drh
    jeä jk w;r" ix{dj ksYaYío jk ;rug" úia;drh wvq fõ'

    Frequency modulation (FM) / ixLHd; uQ¾ckh
    In frequency modulation, the frequency of the carrier signal is varied in proportion to the amplitude of the
    information (baseband) signal, while the amplitude and phase of the carrier remain constant.
    ixLHd; uqyq¾ckfha§" jdyl ix{dfõ ixLHd;h f;dr;=re ix{dfõ úia;drhg iudkqmd;slj fjkia
    lrk w;r" jdylfha úia;drh iy l,dj ksh;j mj;S'

    The louder the information signal, the more the frequency of the carrier signal shifts.
    f;dr;=re ix{dfõ Yíoh jeä jk ;rug jdyl ix{dfõ ixLHd;h jeä fõ'

    Phase modulation (PM) / l,d uQ¾ckh
    In phase modulation, the phase of the carrier signal is varied in proportion to the amplitude of the
    information (baseband) signal, while the carrier’s amplitude and frequency remain constant.
    l,d uQ¾ckfha§" jdyl ix{dfõ l,dj f;dr;=re ix{dfõ úia;drhg iudkqmd;slj fjkia lrk w;r"
    jdylfha úia;drh iy ixLHd;h ksh;j mj;S'

    As the amplitude of the information signal changes, the phase of the carrier shifts accordingly.
    f;dr;=re ix{dfõ úia;drh fjkia jk úg" jdylfha l,dj tA wkqj fjkia fõ'

    Pulse Code Modulation (PCM) / iamkaos; fla; uQ¾ckh
    Pulse Code Modulation encoding is a technique used to convert analog signals into digital signals.
    iamkaos; fla; uQ¾ckh hkq m%;siu ix{d wxls; ix{d njg mßj¾;kh lsÍu i|yd Ndú;d lrk
    ;dlaIKhls'
RAVINDU BANDARANAYAKE                               -13-                                           #ictfromabc


    Time Division Modulation (TDM) / ld, fnÿï uQ¾ckh
    Time Division Modulation (TDM) is a technique used in communication systems to transmit multiple
    signals over a single communication channel by dividing the time into several slots. Each signal is
    assigned a specific time slot, and the signals take turns using the channel.
    ld, fnÿï uQ¾ckh hkq ikaksfõok moaO;sj, Ndú;d lrk ;dlaIKhls" th ld,h ;õ lsysmhlg
    fn§fuka ;ks ikaksfõok kd,sldjla yryd nyq ix{d iïfm%aIKh lrhs' iEu ix{djlgu ksYaÑ;
    ld, mr;rhla mjrd we;s w;r" ix{d udrefjka udrejg kd,sldj Ndú;d lrhs'

    Correct Answer / ksjerÈ ms<s;=r

    (3) Pulse Code Modulation (PCM)
    iamkaos; fla; uQ¾ckh

    Why PCM is correct / PCM ksjerÈ jkafka wehs
    Pulse Code Modulation (PCM) is a technique that converts analog signals (continuous waveforms) into
    digital signals (binary data). It is widely used in digital audio systems, telephone networks, and other
    communication systems.
    iamkaos; fla; uQ¾ckh hkq m%;siu ix{d ^wLKav ;rx. wdldr& äðg,a ix{d ^oaúuh o;a;& njg
    mßj¾;kh lrk ;dlaIKhls' th äðg,a Y%jH moaO;s" ÿrl:k cd, iy wfkl=;a ikaksfõok
    moaO;sj, nyq,j Ndú;d fõ'

    PCM works in 3 main steps / PCM m%Odk mshjr 3 lska l%shd lrhs
    Sampling – The analog signal is sampled at regular time intervals.
    kshe§u - m%;siu ix{dj ks;sm;d ld, mr;rhkays§ idïm, ,nd .kS'

    Quantization – Each sample is approximated to the nearest value from a finite set of levels.
    m%udKlrKh - iEu ksheÈhlau iSñ; uÜgï iuQyhlska wdikak;u w.hg wdikak fõ'

    Encoding – Each quantized value is encoded into a binary number.
    fla;kh lsÍu - iEu m%udKd;aul w.hlau oaúuh wxlhlg fla;kh lr we;'

    Result: The continuous analog waveform becomes a stream of binary digits (digital signal).
    m%;sM,h( wLKav m%;siu ;rx. wdlD;sh oaúuh b,lalï m%jdyhla njg m;afõ ^äðg,a ix{d&'

 13. A computer in a network is configured with the IP address 192.248.16.91 and the subnet mask
     255.255.255.128. Which of the following IP addresses cannot be assigned to a computer in the
     same network?
     cd,hl mj;sk mß.Klhla 192.248.16.91 hk IP ,smskh iy 255.255.255.128 hk Wmcd,
     wdjrKh (subnet mask) u.ska úkHdi.; lr we;' fuu cd,fha mj;sk mß.Klhla i|yd ,ndÈh
     fkdyelafla my; fmkajd we;s ljr IP ,smskhla o?

    4) 192.148.16.75                              5) 192.248.16.120

    Answer: 1 and 4)

    IP Address / IP ,smskh : 192.248.16.91
    Subnet Mask / Wmcd, wdjrKh : 255.255.255.128 (equivalent to /25)

    Step 1
    Understand the Subnet Mask (/25) / Wmcd, wdjrKh (/25) f;areï .kak
    The subnet mask /25 (255.255.255.128) means
    Wmcd, wdjrKh /25 (255.255.255.128) hkafkka woyia jkafka

    First 25 bits are for the network
    m<uq ìgq 25 cd,h i|yd fõ

    Remaining 7 bits (32 - 25 = 7) are for hosts
    b;sß ìgq 7 bits (32 - 25 = 7) Odrl i|yd fõ

    So each subnet can have 27 =128 IP addresses
    tneúka iEu Wmcd,hlgu IP ,smsk 27 =128 la ;sìh yelsh

    •   1 is reserved for network address / 1 cd, ,smskh i|yd fjka lr we;
    •   1 is reserved for broadcast address / 1 úldYk ,smskh i|yd fjka lr we;
    •   That leaves 126 valid host addresses / tu.ska j,x.= i;aldrl ,smsk 126 la b;sß fõ

    Step 2
    Find the network range for 192.248.16.91/25
    192.248.16.91/25 i|yd cd, mrdih fidhd .kak
    Since each subnet spans 128 IP addresses / tla tla Wmcd,h IP ,smsk 128 la úysfok neúka
    The subnet ranges go in steps of 128 / Wmcd, mrdihka 128 mshjr j,ska .uka lrhs

    First subnet: 192.248.16.0 to 192.248.16.127
    m<uq Wmcd,h( 192.248.16.0 isg 192.248.16.127 olajd

    Second subnet: 192.248.16.128 to 192.248.16.255
    fojk Wmcd,h( 192.248.16.128 isg 192.248.16.255 olajd
RAVINDU BANDARANAYAKE                              -15-                        #ictfromabc


    The IP 192.248.16.91 falls into the first subnet
    192.248.16.91 IP tl m<uq Wmcd,hg wh;a fõ

    Network Address / cd, ,smskh: 192.248.16.0
    Broadcast Address / úldYk ,smskh: 192.248.16.127

    j,x.= i;aldrl IP: 192.248.16.1 isg 192.248.16.126 olajd

    Let's evaluate each option / wms tla tla úl,amh we.hSug ,la lruq
    Option/ úl,amh 1)

    tfiau 192.248.16.0/25 ;=<

    Different network altogether / iïmQ¾Kfhkau fjkia cd,hla

    Within 192.248.16.0/25
    192.248.16.0/25 we;=<;

 17. What is the correct CSS rule to set the background colour of a web page to yellow?

 18. Which of the following statements is correct with respect to the Transmission Control Protocol
     (TCP)?
     iïfma%IK md,k kshudj,sh (TCP) iïnkaOfhka my; i|yka j.ka;s w;=frka ksjerÈ jkafka l=ula
     o?
    1) TCP is a network layer protocol / TCP hkq cd, ia:r (network layer) kshudj,shls'
    2) TCP guarantees that each byte sent is received at the receiver.
       hjkq ,nk iEu nhsghlau .%ylhd fj; ,eîu TCP u.ska iy;sl lrhs'
    3) Only one application at a time can use TCP in a computer.
       tla mß.Klhla ;=< TCP Ndú; l< yelafla tla jrlg tla fhÿulg muKs'
    4) HTTP uses TCP / HTTP, TCP Ndú; lrhs'
    5) TCP uses User Datagram Protocol (UDP) as the transport protocol.
       TCP úiska m%jdyk kshudj,sh f,i User Datagram kshudj,sh (UDP) Ndú; lrkq ,nhs'

    Answer: 2 and 4)

          Feature                         TCP                                     UDP
     Connection Type    Connection-oriented                       Connectionless
                        iïnkaO;d-keUqre                           iïnkaO;d rys;

 19. A LAN uses the subnet mask 255.255.240.0. How many different IP addresses can be assigned to
     devices in this LAN?
     ia:dkSh fmfoia cd,hla (LAN) 255.255.240.0 hk Wmcd, wdjrKh (subnet mask) Ndú; lrhs' fuu
     cd,fha mj;sk Wml%u i|yd Ndú; l< yels tlsfklg fjkia IP ,smsk fldmuK ixLHdjla mj;skafka
     o?
    1) 254                   2) 256               3) 1024         4) 2046                5) 4094

    Answer: 5)

    To solve this, we need to calculate the number of assignable IP addresses based on the given subnet
    mask
    fuh úi£u i|yd" ,nd § we;s Wmcd, wdjrKh u; mokïj mejßh yels IP ,smsk .Kk .Kkh
    l< hq;=h

    Subnet Mask / Wmcd, wdjrKh : 255.255.240.0

    Step 1
    Convert the subnet mask to CIDR (/x) format
    Wmcd, wdjrKh CIDR (/x) wdlD;shg mßj¾;kh lrkak

    Convert 255.255.240.0 to binary
    255.255.240.0 oaúuh njg mßj¾;kh lrkak

    255    = 11111111
    255    = 11111111
    240    = 11110000
    0      = 00000000

    This gives / fuh ,nd fokafka
    11111111.11111111.11110000.00000000 → total 20 bits for the network.
    11111111.11111111.11110000.00000000 → cd,h i|yd uq¿ ìgq 20.

    So this is /20 subnet mask
    Tneúka fuh /20 Wmcd, wdjrKhls

    Step 2
    Calculate number of host addresses / i;aldrl ,smsk .Kk .Kkh lrkak

    Total bits = 32
    Host bits = 32 - 20 = 12 bits
    Number of addresses = 212 = 4096

    But 2 addresses are reserved / kuq;a ,smsk 2la fjka lr we;

    1 for the network address / cd, ,smskh i|yd 1
    1 for the broadcast address / úldYk ,smskh i|yd 1

    Usable addresses = 4096 – 2 = 4094
    Ndú;d l< yels ,smsk = 4096 – 2 = 4094

    Therefore, option 5) is the correct answer.
    tneúka" 5) úl,amh ksjerÈ ms<s;=r fõ'
RAVINDU BANDARANAYAKE                              -23-                                          #ictfromabc

 21. Consider the following terms related to computer systems:
     mß.Kl moaO;s yd iïnkaO my; i|yka mo i,ld n,kak(
    A – Malware / wksYag uDÿldx.                     B – Hardware /oDVdx.
    C – Software / uDÿldx.                           D – Liveware / Ôjdx.
    Which of the above are basic components of a computer system?
    mß.Kl moaO;shl uQ,sl ixrpl jkafka by; i|yka oE w;=frka ljrla o?
    1) A and B only.    2) A and C only.    3) A and D only. 4) B and C only.      5) B, C and D only.
       A yd B muKs.        A yd C muKs.        A yd D muKs.     B yd C muKs.          B, C yd D muKs.

    Explanation / meyeÈ,s lsÍu

    A – Malware/ wksYag uDÿldx.
    Malware stands for malicious software. It includes harmful programs like viruses, worms, trojans, and
    spyware that are designed to damage or disrupt computer systems. Although malware is a type of
    software, it is not considered a basic component of a computer system.
    wksIag uDÿldx. hkq ydkslr uDÿldx. hkakhs' mß.Kl moaO;s j,g ydks lsÍug fyda lvdlmam,a
    lsÍug ks¾udKh lr we;s ffjri" mKqjka" fg%dacka iy T;a;= uDÿldx. jeks ydkslr jevigyka
    thg we;=<;a fõ' wksIag uDÿldx. j¾.hla jqjo" th mß.Kl moaO;shl uQ,sl wx.hla f,i
    fkdie,fla'

    B – Hardware/ oDVdx.
    Hardware refers to the physical components of a computer system, such as the monitor, keyboard,
    processor, RAM, hard drive, and motherboard. It is a core, basic component of any computer system, as
    it forms the tangible infrastructure on which all software operates.
    oDVdx. hkq fudksgrh" h;=remqjrej" ilikh" RAM" oDV ;eáh iy ujq mqjrej jeks mß.Kl
    moaO;shl fN!;sl ixrpl fõ' th ´kEu mß.Kl moaO;shl yrh" uQ,sl ixrplhls" ukao th
    ish¨u uDÿldx. l%shd;aul jk iam¾YH há;, myiqlï idohs'

    C – Software/ uDÿldx.
    Software includes the programs and operating systems that run on the hardware. It tells the computer
    what to do and how to do it. Examples include Windows, Linux, Microsoft Office, and web browsers.
    Software is a basic component of a computer system because it is essential for functioning.
    uDÿldx.hg oDVdx. u; l%shd;aul jk jevigyka iy fufyhqï moaO;s we;=<;a fõ' th
    mß.Klhg l< hq;= foa iy th lrkafka flfiao hkak lshhs' WodyrK f,i Windows, Linux,
    Microsoft Office, iy fjí n%õir we;=<;a fõ' uDÿldx. hkq mß.Kl moaO;shl uQ,sl wx.hls"
    ukao th l%shd;aul ùug w;HjYH fõ'

    D – Liveware/ Ôjdx.
    Liveware refers to the humans who use or operate the computer system. This includes users,
    programmers, and technicians. Although not a physical or digital part, liveware is considered a basic
    component because a computer cannot function meaningfully without human interaction.
    iÔù uDÿldx. hkq mß.Kl moaO;sh Ndú;d lrk fyda l%shd;aul lrk ñksiqka h' fuhg
    mßYS,lhska" l%uf,aLlhska iy ld¾ñlhka we;=<;a fõ' fN!;sl fyda äðg,a fldgila fkdjqk;a" iÔù
    uDÿldx. uQ,sl wx.hla f,i i,lkq ,nkafka ñksia wka;¾l%shd fkdue;sj mß.Klhlg w¾:j;a
    f,i l%shd l< fkdyels neúks'

 22. In a public key encryption system, the private key of a person x is given by the function priv(x) and
     the public key is given by pub(x).
     fmdÿ h;=re .=ma; fla;l moaO;shl (public key encryption system) § x kï mqoa.,hl=f.a fm!oa.,sl
     h;=r (private key), priv(x) hk Y%s;fhka yd fmdÿ h;=r (public key), pub(x) hk Y%s;fhka o fokq
     ,efí'
    Consider the following statements:
    my; i|yka j.ka;s i,ld n,kak(
    A. pub(x) is used to encrypt a message that can only be decrypted using priv(x).
       priv(x) Ndú; lsÍu u.ska muKla úfla;kh (decrypt) l< yels fia mKsjqvhla .=ma; fla;kh
       lsÍu i|yd pub(x) Ndú; lrhs'
    B. pub(x) is used to sign a message to be sent to x.
       x fj; hjk mKsjqvhl w;aika ;eîu (sign) i|yd pub(x) Ndú; lrhs'
    C. A message encrypted using pub(x) can be decrypted using pub(x).
       pub(x) Ndú;fhka .=ma; fla;kh lrkq ,enQ mKsjqvhla pub(x) Ndú;fhka úfla;kh l< yels fõ'
    Which of the above statement(s) is/are correct?
    by; j.ka;s w;=frka ksjerÈ jkafka"

    1) A only.          2) B only.          3) C only.       4) A and B only.     5) B and C only.
       A muKs.             B muKs.             C muKs.          A yd B muKs.         B yd C muKs.
    Answer: 1)

    In public key cryptography / fmdÿ h;=re .=ma; fla;kfha§

    Each person x has:
    iEu mqoa.,fhl=gu x we;af;a

    •   A public key: pub(x) (shared with everyone)
        fmdÿ h;=rla( pub(x) ^ish¨ fokd iuÕ fnodf.k we;&

    •   A private key: priv(x) (kept secret)
        fm!oa.,sl h;=rla( priv(x) ^ryis.;j ;nd we;&

    There are two main uses of public key cryptography:
    fmdÿ h;=re .=ma; fla;kfha§ m%Odk Ndú;hka folla ;sfí(

    Confidentiality (Encryption) / ryiHNdjh ^fla;kh&
    Encrypt with recipient's public key → only recipient’s private key can decrypt.
    ,nkakdf.a fmdÿ h;=r iuÕ fla;kh lrkak → ,nkakdf.a mqoa.,sl h;=rg muKla úfla;kh l<
    yelsh'

    Ensures only the intended recipient can read the message.
    wfmalaIs; ,nkakdg muKla mKsúvh lshúh yels nj iy;sl lrhs'

    Authentication / Digital Signatures / i;Hdmkh $ äðg,a w;aika
    Sign with sender's private key → anyone can verify it using sender’s public key.
    hjkakdf.a mqoa.,sl h;=r iuÕ w;aika lrkak → ´kEu flfkl=g hjkakdf.a fmdÿ h;=r
    Ndú;fhka th i;Hdmkh l< yelsh'

    Ensures authenticity and integrity.
    i;H;dj iy wLKav;dj iy;sl lrhs'
RAVINDU BANDARANAYAKE                               -26-                                          #ictfromabc


    Statement Analysis / m%ldYk úYaf,aIKh

    Statement / m%ldYh A)
    Correct / ksjerÈ
    This is the standard use for ensuring confidentiality.
    ryiHNdjh iy;sl lsÍu i|yd iïu; Ndú;h fuhhs'

    Encrypt with recipient’s public key → only they (with priv(x)) can decrypt.
    ,nkakdf.a fmdÿ h;=r iuÕ fla;kh lrkak → Tjqkag ^priv(x) iuÕ& muKla fla;kh l< yelsh'

    Statement / m%ldYh B)
    Inorrect / jerÈ
    Signing is done with the sender’s private key, not the recipient’s public key.
    w;aika lsÍu ,nkakdf.a fmdÿ h;=frka fkdj" hjkakdf.a mqoa.,sl h;=frka isÿ flf¾'

    You never "sign a message with pub(x)".
    Tn lsis úfgl;a ¶pub(x) iuÕ mKsúvhla w;aika lrkak¶ ke;'

    Statement / m%ldYh C)
    Inorrect / jerÈ
    Only the corresponding private key priv(x) can decrypt what was encrypted with pub(x).
    Pub(x) iuÕ fla;kh l< foa úfla;kh l< yelafla wkqrEm mqoa.,sl h;=r priv(x) g muKs'

    Therefore, option 1) is the correct answer.
    tneúka" 1) úl,amh ksjerÈ ms<s;=r fõ'

    Statement Analysis / m%ldYk úYaf,aIKh

    Statement / m%ldYh A)
    Correct. Each processor (CPU) has its own instruction set architecture (ISA), meaning it can execute
    only machine code (binary instructions) specifically designed for that processor. So, this statement is
    true.
    ksjerÈhs' iEu ilikhlgu (CPU) ;ukaf.au Wmfoia lÜg, wdlD;s ks¾udK Ys,amh (ISA) we;"

    wi;Hhls'

    Statement / m%ldYh D)

 26. Consider the following statements on Dynamic Random Access Memory (DRAM) and Static
     Random Access Memory (SRAM):
     .;sl iiïNdú m%fõY u;l (DRAM) yd iaÓ;sl iiïNdú m%fõY u;l (SRAM) iïnkaOfhka my;
     j.ka;s i,ld n,kak(
    A. Registers are made of DRAM / frðia;r idod we;af;a DRAM u.sks'
    B. DRAM is faster than SRAM / SRAM g jvd DRAM fõ.j;a fõ'
    C. DRAM is more dense than SRAM / SRAM g jvd DRAM .yk (dense) fõ'

    Which of the above statement(s) is/are correct?
    by; j.ka;s w;=frka ksjerÈ jkafka ljrla o?

    1) A only.          2) B only.          3) C only.       4) A and B only.     5) B and C only.
       A muKs.             B muKs.             C muKs.          A yd B muKs.         B yd C muKs.

    Answer: 3)

    Explanation / meyeÈ,s lsÍu

    Statement / m%ldYh A)
    Registers are not made of DRAM. Registers are high-speed, volatile memory units inside the CPU, and
    they are made using SRAM, not DRAM. This is because SRAM is much faster and more suitable for
    rapid, frequent access, which is essential for CPU operations. DRAM is slower and requires frequent
    refreshing, making it unsuitable for registers. Therefore, this statement is factually incorrect.
    frðiag¾ DRAM j,ska idod ke;' frðiag¾ hkq CPU ;=< we;s wêfõ.S" kIH u;l tAll jk w;r
    tAjd DRAM fkdj SRAM Ndú;fhka idod we;' fuhg fya;=j SRAM b;d fõ.j;a jk w;r fõ.j;a"
    ks;r m%fõY ùu i|yd jvd;a iqÿiq jk w;r th CPU fufyhqï i|yd w;HjYH fõ' DRAM ukao.dó
    jk w;r ks;r kejqï lsÍu wjYH jk w;r th frðiag¾ i|yd kqiqÿiq fõ' tneúka" fuu m%ldYh
    i;H jYfhkau jerÈh'
RAVINDU BANDARANAYAKE                                -30-                                           #ictfromabc


    Statement / m%ldYh B)
    This statement is also false. In reality, SRAM is significantly faster than DRAM. That’s why SRAM is
    used for cache memory, which needs very fast access, while DRAM is used for main memory (RAM)
    due to its cost-effectiveness and density. DRAM’s speed is limited because each memory cell needs to
    be refreshed regularly, whereas SRAM retains data as long as power is supplied. So, DRAM is slower,
    not faster, than SRAM.
    fuu m%ldYh o wi;Hhls' kQ;kfha § SRAM DRAM g jvd ie,lsh hq;= f,i fõ.j;a fõ' b;d
    fõ.j;a m%fõYhla wjYH jk ksys; u;lh i|yd SRAM Ndú;d lrkafka tneúks" tys msßjeh-
    M,odhS;djh iy >k;ajh ksid DRAM m%Odk u;lh RAM i|yd Ndú;d fõ' DRAM ys fõ.h iSñ;
    jkafka iEu u;l tAllhlau ks;sm;d kejqï l< hq;= ksid jk w;r SRAM n,h imhk ;dla l,a
    o;a; r|jd .kS' tneúka" DRAM SRAM g jvd ukao.dó fõ" fõ.j;a fkdfõ'

    Statement / m%ldYh C)
    This statement is true. DRAM is built using a single capacitor and transistor per bit, while SRAM uses six
    transistors per bit. This makes DRAM more compact and dense, allowing for higher memory capacities
    in smaller physical spaces at a lower cost. That’s why DRAM is ideal for system RAM, where large
    capacity is needed, and SRAM is used in smaller but faster applications, like CPU caches.
    fuu m%ldYh i;Hhls' DRAM ìÜ tllg ;ks Odß;%lhla iy g%dkaisiagrhla Ndú;fhka f.dvk.d
    we;s w;r SRAM ìÜ tllg g%dkaisiagr yhla Ndú;d lrhs' fuh DRAM jvd;a ixhqla; yd >k njg
    m;a lrhs" l=vd fN!;sl wjldYhkays wvq úhoulska by< u;l Odß;djla i|yd bv i,ihs' úYd,
    Odß;djla wjYH jk moaO;s RAM i|yd DRAM jvd;a iqÿiq jkafka tneúks" iy SRAM CPU ksys;
    u;l jeks l=vd kuq;a fõ.j;a fhÿïj, Ndú;d fõ'

    Therefore, option 3) is the correct answer.
    tneúka" 3) úl,amh ksjerÈ ms<s;=r fõ'

    crucial for effective collaboration, especially in progress review meetings involving presentations, charts,
    or reports. Additionally, international calls may lead to high costs and time zone challenges. Thus, this

 •   Questions 28 to 31 are based on an algorithm represented by the following flow chart.
     wxl 28 isg 31 f;la m%Yak i|yd my; .e,Sï igyk u.ska fmkajd we;s we,af.dß;uh mdol fõ'

                                                Start


                                          Read a value for X

                                                    Yes

                                               Print T


                                                End

 28. Consider the following statements:
     my; j.ka;s i,ld n,kak(
     A. This algorithm takes only a single input / fuu we,af.dß;uh tla wdodkhla muKla ,nd .kshs'
     B. This algorithm does not have any repetition(loop).
        fuu we,af.dß;uhg lsisÿ mqkrdj¾;khla (repetition/loop) we;=<;a fkdfõ'
     C. If the user inputs -1 for X, the algorithm will not terminate.
        mßYS,lhd X i|yd -1 wdodkh l<fyd;a we,af.dß;uh k;r fkdfõ'
     D. When the user inputs for X, the algorithm will not terminate till the user enters another value.
        mßYS,lhd X i|yd 1 wdodkh l<fyd;a mßYS,lhd h<s;a w.hla we;=<;a lrk ;=re we,af.dß;uh
        k;r fkdfõ'
     Which of the above statement(s) is/are correct?
     by; j.ka;s w;=frka ksjeros jkafka ljrla o?

     1) A only.        2) A and B only.   3) A and D only. 4) B and C only.     5) C and D only.
        A muKs.           A yd B muKs.       A yd D muKs.     B yd C muKs.         C yd D muKs.

     Answer: 5)

     Let’s breakdown the given flowchart / § we;s .e,Sï igyk i,ld n,uq

     User inputs a value for X.
     mßYS,lhd X i|yd w.hla we;=<;a lrhs'

     Set values for N and T as N = 1 and T = 0.
     N iy T i|yd w.hka N = 1 iy T = 0 f,i i,lhs'
RAVINDU BANDARANAYAKE                                -33-                                           #ictfromabc


    Inside the loop / Æmh we;=<;

    •   A value for Y is taken from the user.
        Y i|yd w.hla mßYS,lhdf.ka ,nd .kS'

    •   Y is added to T.
        Y, T g tl;= lrkq ,efí'

    •   Then, the algorithm checks whether N is equal to X.
        túg" we,af.dß;uh N, X g iudko hkak mÍlaId lrhs'

    •   If ‘No’, increment N and the algorithm is repeated.
        ‘No’ kï" N jeä lr we,af.dß;uh kej;;a isÿ flf¾'

    •   If ‘Yes’, print T and the algorithm ends.
        ‘Yes’ kï" T uqøKh lr we,af.dß;uh wjika fõ'

    Evaluating each statement / tla tla m%ldYh meyeÈ,s lsÍu

    Statement / m%ldYh A
    Incorrect. It takes multiple inputs: one for X and X values for Y.
    jerÈhs' thg wdodk lsysmhla wjYH fõ( X i|yd tlla iy Y i|yd X w.hka'

    Statement / m%ldYh B
    Incorrect. It clearly has a loop (while N ≠ X).
    jerÈhs' thg meyeÈ,sju Æmhla we; (N ≠ X f;la).

    Statement / m%ldYh C
    Correct. If X = -1, the condition N = X will never be satisfied because N starts at 1 and increases. It is an
    infinite loop.
    ksjerÈhs' X = -1 kï" N = X ;;a;ajh lsis úfgl;a ;Dma;su;a fkdjkq we;" ukao N, 1 ka wdrïN ù
    jeä fõ' th wkka; Æmhla fõ'

    Statement / m%ldYh D
    Correct. After entering X, it waits for inputs for Y repeatedly until N = X.
    ksjerÈhs' X we;=¿ lsÍfuka miq" th N = X f;la kej; kej;;a Y i|yd wdodk i|yd /£ isà'

    So, the correct statements are C and D only.
    tuksid" ksjerÈ m%ldY C iy D muKs'

    Therefore, option 5) is the correct answer.
    tneúka" 5) úl,amh ksjerÈ ms<s;=r fõ'

 29. The algorithm represented by the flowchart is considered as a poor algorithm because it does not
     .e,Sï igyk u.ska ksrEms; we,af.dß;uh ÿ¾j, we,af.dß;uhla fia i,lkq ,nkafka"
    1) terminate for some input values / th iuyr wdodk w.hkaj, § fkdkj;sk ksid h'
    2) contain finite number of steps / thg ksYaÑ; mshjr ixLHdjla fkdue;s ksid h'
    3) specify the next step to be performed at least for a one step of the algorithm.
       B<Õ mshjr .ek i|ykla fkdue;s wvq u jYfhka tla mshjrlaj;a tys we;s ksid h'
    4) consist of a sequence of steps / th mshjr wkql%uhlska iukaú; fkdjk ksid h'
    5) contain any variable type declarations
       tys lsisu úp,H m%rEm (variable type) y÷kajd§ula we;=<;a fkdjk ksid h'

    Answer: 1)

    Explanation / meyeÈ,s lsÍu

    Option / úl,amh 1)
    Correct. Here, N is equal to 1. When a value less than N is entered to X, we cannot satisfy the N = X
    condition by incrementing the value of N. So, it becomes an infinite loop.
    ksjerÈhs' fuys§" N, 1 g iudk fõ' X fj; N g wvq w.hla we;=<;a l< úg" N ys w.h jeä
    lsÍfuka wmg N = X fldkafoaish imqrd,sh fkdyel' tneúka" th wkka; Æmhla njg m;afõ'

    Option / úl,amh 2)
    Incorrect. That is not a problem. Finite steps are desirable.
    jerÈhs' th .eg¿jla fkdfõ' iSñ; mshjr fhda.H fõ'

    Option / úl,amh 3)
    Incorrect. Here, the next steps are clearly specified for every step of the flowchart till the ‘end’.
    jerÈhs' fuys§" .e,Sï igyfka iEu mshjrla i|ydu ‘end’ olajd B<Õ mshjr meyeÈ,sj olajd we;'

    Option / úl,amh 4)
    Incorrect. It clearly does consist of a sequence of steps.
    jerÈhs' th meyeÈ,sju mshjr wkqms<sfj,lska iukaú; fõ'

    Option / úl,amh 5)
    Incorrect. It is not a reason for calling it poor in algorithmic flowchart conventions.
    jerÈhs' we,af.dß;u m%jdy igyk iïuq;Ska ;=< th ÿ¾j, f,i ye¢kaùug th fya;=jla fkdfõ'

    Therefore, option 1) is the correct answer.
    tneúka" 1) úl,amh ksjerÈ ms<s;=r fõ'

 30. The algorithm terminates
     fuu we,af.dß;uh kj;skafka"
    1) for input value 5 / w.h 5 wdodkh l< miqj h'
    2) when values 0,5,4 are given as input one after the other.
       w.hka 0, 5, 4 tllg miq tlla f,i wdodkh l< miq h'
    3) by printing the value 5 when it is given the input values 2,5,4 one after the other.
       w.hka 2, 5, 4 tllg miq tlla f,i wdodkh l< miq w.h 5 uqøKh lsÍfuks'
    4) by printing the value 4 when it is given the input values 2,5,4 one after the other.
       w.hka 2, 5, 4 tllg miq tlla f,i wdodkh l< miq w.h 4 uqøKh lsÍfuks'
    5) by printing the value 9 when it is given the input values 2,5,4 one after the other.
       w.hka 2, 5, 4 tllg miq tlla f,i wdodkh l< miq w.h 9 uqøKh lsÍfuks'

    Answer: 5)

    Explanation / meyeÈ,s lsÍu

    Option / úl,amh 1)
    This option is unclear, because input/s for Y is/are not mentioned. If 5 is entered to X, then 5 inputs for Y
    will be taken. When N = 5, it terminates.
    fuu úl,amh wmeyeÈ,s h" ukao Y i|yd wdodk$h i|yka lr fkdue;s neúks' X fj; 5 we;=<;a
    l<fyd;a" Y i|yd wdodk 5 la .kq ,efí' N = 5 jk úg" th wjika fõ'

    Option / úl,amh 2)
    Here, N is equal to 1. When 0 is entered to X, we cannot satisfy the N = X condition by incrementing the
    value of N. So, it becomes an infinite loop.
    fuys§" N, 1 g iudk fõ' X fj; 0 we;=<;a l< úg" N ys w.h jeä lsÍfuka wmg N = X
    fldkafoaish imqrd,sh fkdyel' tneúka" th wkka; Æmhla njg m;afõ'

    Option / úl,amh 3)
    Let’s simulate the process and determine the output: / l%shdj,sh wkqlrKh lr m%;sodkh ;SrKh lruq

    At first, 2 is entered as the value of X. (N = 1, T = 0)
    m<uqj" X ys w.h f,i 2 we;=<;a lr we;' (N = 1, T = 0)

    Y1 = 5 → T = T + Y1 = 0 + 5 = 5.
    So, the value of T becomes 5, when 5 is entered to Y.
    tneúka" 5, Y fj; we;=<;a l< úg T ys w.h 5 njg m;afõ'

    As N is not equal to X, another input for Y is taken.
    N, X g iudk fkdjk neúka" Y i|yd ;j;a wdodkhla .kq ,efí'

    Y2 = 4 → T = T + Y2 = 4 + 5 = 9
    Then, the value of T becomes 9, when 4 is entered to Y.
    túg" 4, Y fj; we;=<;a l< úg T ys w.h 9 njg m;afõ'

    So, T is printed as 9, not 5. So, this option is incorrect.
    tneúka" T uqøKh lrkq ,nkafka 5 f,i fkdj 9 f,i h' tneúka" fuu úl,amh jerÈ h'

    Option / úl,amh 4)
    The operation is as same as above. 9 is printed as the result. So, this option is also incorrect.
    fufyhqu by; wdldrhgu fõ' 9 m%;sM,h f,i uqøKh lr we;' tneúka" fuu úl,amh o jerÈ h'
RAVINDU BANDARANAYAKE                                -36-                                          #ictfromabc


    Option / úl,amh 5)
    The operation is as same as in option 3). 9 is printed as the result. So, this is the correct simulation
    making option 5) the correct answer.
    fufyhqu 3) úl,amfha we;s wdldrhgu fõ' 9 m%;sM,h f,i uqøKh lr we;' tneúka" fuh ksjerÈ
    wkqlrKh fõ' úl,amh 5) ksjerÈ ms<s;=r fõ'

    Therefore, option 5) is the correct answer.
    tneúka" 5) úl,amh ksjerÈ ms<s;=r fõ'

 31. Which of the following Python programs implements the behaviour of the flowchart?
     .e,Sï igyfka yeisÍu l%shdjg kxjkafka my; i|yka l=uk mhs;ka l%uf,aLfhka o?

    1) x = int(input("Enter a value : "))                   2)   x = int(input("Enter a value : "))
         n = 1                                                   n = 1
         t = 0                                                   t = 0
         while n <= x:                                           while n <= x:
           y = int(input("Enter the next value: "))                y =int(input("Enter the next value: "))
           t = t + y                                               t = t + y
           n = n + 1                                               n = n + 1
         print(t)                                                  print(t)


    3)   x = int(input("Enter a value : "))                 4)   x = int(input("Enter a value : "))
         n = 1                                                   n = 1
         t = 0                                                   t = 0
         iterate = True                                          while n != x:
         while n != x:                                             y = int(input("Enter the next value: "))
           y = int(input("Enter the next value: "))                t = t + y
           t= t + y                                                n = n + 1
           n = n + 1                                               print(t)
         print(t)

    5)   x = int(input("Enter a value : "))
         n = 1
         t = 0
         iterate = True
         while iterate:
           y = int(input("Enter the next value: "))
           t = t + y
           if n == x:
             iterate = False
           else:
             n = n + 1
         print(t)

    Answer: 5)

    Explanation / meyeÈ,s lsÍu

    Option / úl,amh 1)
    Loop runs while n <= x, so it also runs when n == x. So, this is not identical to the flowchart. It does not
    stop exactly when n == x, it goes until n > x. So, it performs one extra iteration, when it should be
    terminated.
    Æmh n <= x jk úg l%shd;aul jk w;r" th n == x jk úgo l%shd;aul fõ' tneúka" fuh .e,Sï
    igykg iudk fkdfõ' th n == x jk úg k;r fkdfõ" th n > x jk f;la hhs' tneúka" th
    wjika l< hq;= úg th tla wu;r mqkrdj¾;khla isÿ lrhs'

    Option / úl,amh 2)
    Same problem as in option 1). The program runs until n > x, having an extra iteration. print(t) is placed
    incorrectly. It should be placed after the loop. This makes the algorithm prints sum in every iteration.
    úl,am 1) ys we;s .eg¿ju fõ' jevigyk n > x jk f;la l%shd;aul jk w;r" wu;r
    mqkrdj¾;khla we;' print(t) jerÈ f,i ;nd we;' th Æmhg miqj ;eìh hq;h                 = ' fuh
    we,af.dß;uh iEu mqkrdj¾;khlu tl;=j uqøKh lrhs'
RAVINDU BANDARANAYAKE                                -37-                                            #ictfromabc


    Option / úl,amh 3)
    The variable named ‘iterate’ is never used. And also, the loop stops when n == x, because the condition
    is checked at the beginning of the loop not like in the flowchart. So, it runs only x - 1 times. It lacks one
    iteration.
    ‘iterate’ kï úp,Hh lsis úfgl;a Ndú;d fkdfõ' ;jo" n == x jk úg Æmh k;r fõ" ukao ;;ajh
    m%jdy igyfka fuka fkdj Æmfha wdrïNfha § mÍlaId lr we;' tneúka" th x - 1 j;djla muKla
    Odjkh fõ' thg tla mqkrdj¾;khla wvq fõ'

    Option / úl,amh 4)
    Same logic problem as option 3). It terminates early (x - 1 times). Also, print(t) is inside loop. It should
    be placed after the loop. This makes the algorithm prints sum in every iteration.
    úl,amh 3) f,iu ;d¾lsl .eg¿jla we;' th l,ska wjika fõ ^x - 1 j;djla&' tfiau" print(t) Æmh
    ;=< we;' th Æmhg miqj ;eìh hq;=h' fuh we,af.dß;uh iEu mqkrdj¾;khlu tl;=j uqøKh
    lrhs'

    Option / úl,amh 5)
    This program takes input for X, initializes N = 1, T = 0. Then, within the loop in every iteration, it takes
    a value for Y. Add it to T. Then, if N == X, the repetition is stopped and the value of T is printed. If not,
    N is incremented. print(t) is placed correctly. So, it executes only once, after the loop. So, this is the
    correct implementation of the flowchart.
    fuu jevigyk X i|yd wdodkh ,nd .kS" N = 1, T = 0 wdrïN lrhs' bkamiq" iEu
    mqkrdj¾;khl§u Æmh ;=<" th Y i|yd w.hla .kS' th T g tl;= lrkak' bkamiq" N == X kï"
    mqkrdj¾;kh k;r lr T ys w.h uqøKh flf¾' tfia fkdfõ kï" N jeä lrkq ,efí' print(t)
    ksjerÈj ;nd we;' tneúka th Æmfhka miqj tla jrla muKla l%shd;aul fõ' tneúka" fuh .e,Sï
    igyfka ksjerÈ l%shd;aul lsÍuhs'

    Therefore, option 5) is the correct answer.
    tneúka" 5) úl,amh ksjerÈ ms<s;=r fõ'

 32. Consider the following statement regarding an Automatic Teller Machine (ATM) of a bank:
     “System shall dispense cash in less than 10 seconds.”
     Which of the following is correct with respect to the above statement?
     nexl=jl iajhxl%Sh fg,¾ hka;%hla (ATM) iïnkaOfhka my; fmkajd we;s j.ka;sh i,lkak:
     “moaO;sh u.ska uqo,a ,nd §u ;;amr 10 lg jvd wvq ld,hl § l< hq;=u h'”
     by; j.ka;sh iïnkaOfhka ksjerÈ jkafka my; i|yka ljrla o?

    1) This is an essential non-functional requirement / fuh w;HjYH ld¾hnoaO fkdjk wjYH;djls'
    2) This is a nice to have non-functional requirement
       fuh we;akï losu ld¾hnoaO fkdjk wjYH;djls'
    3) This is an essential functional requirement / fuh w;HjYH ld¾hnoaO wjYH;djls'

    Answer: 1)

    Explanation / meyeÈ,s lsÍu

    Option / úl,amh 1)

    fuys ld¾hh jkafka( uqo,a fnod yeÍu → fuh ld¾hnoaO wjYH;djhls'

    The time constraint ("in less than 10 seconds") specifies a performance measure → this makes it a non-
    functional requirement.

    wjYH;djhla njg m;a lrhs'
RAVINDU BANDARANAYAKE                                -38-                                           #ictfromabc



    Since this time constraint likely affects customer satisfaction, queue length, and system usability, it is
    considered essential for effective functioning and user experience.

    l%shdldß;ajh iy mßYS,l w;aoelSu i|yd th w;HjYH hehs ie,fla'

    Option / úl,amh 2)
    This timing is not just a luxury—it affects core user satisfaction and system performance. So it's not

    l%shdldß;ajhg n,mdhs' tneúka th úl,am fkdfõ'

    ld¾hnoaO wjYH;d moaO;sh l< hq;= foa ks¾jpkh lrhs ^Wod( uqo,a fnod yeÍu&'

    th ld¾hnoaO wjYH;djhla fkdfõ'

    C ixrpl ksrEmKh lrkafka wkqms<sfj<sk"a

 34. Which of the following statements is correct with respect to openness and closeness of a system?
     moaO;shl újD;Ndjh yd ixjD;Ndjh iïnkaOfhka my; i|yka l=uk j.ka;sh ksjerÈ jkafka o?
    1) An Automatic Teller Machine of a bank should be a close system.
       nexl=jl iajhxl%Sh fg,¾ hka;%hla ixpD; moaO;shla úh hq;= h'
    2) A general purpose computer can be considered as an open system.
       fmdÿ Ndú; mß.Klhla (general purpose computer) újD; moaO;shla fia ie,lsh yels h'
    3) Human blood circulatory system is an open system.
       ñksia reêr ixirK moaO;sh újD; moaO;shls'
    4) A mobile phone is a close system / cx.u ÿrl:kh ixjD; moaO;shls'
    5) A solar power generation system is a close system.
       iQ¾h n, ckk moaO;shla (Solar power generation system) ixjD; moaO;shls'

    Answer: 2)

    To answer this question, we need to recall the basic concepts of open and closed systems:
    fuu m%Yakhg ms<s;=re §ug" újD; iy ixjD; moaO;sj, uQ,sl ixl,am wms isysm;a l< hq;=hs(

    Open System / újD; moaO;sh
    Interacts with its environment. It receives inputs and delivers outputs. Most real-world systems
    (biological, social, information) are open.
    tys mßirh iuÕ wka;¾ l%shd lrhs' th wdodk ,nd .kakd w;r m%;sodkhka ,nd fohs' fndfyda
    ienE f,dal moaO;s ^Ôj úoHd;aul" iudÔh" f;dr;=re& újD;hs'

    Closed System / ixjD; moaO;sh
    Does not interact with the environment. It is self-contained, isolated. Almost no real system is fully
    closed - only theoretical constructs or highly controlled systems.
    mßirh iuÕ wka;¾ l%shd fkdlrhs' th iajhx wka;¾.;" yqol,d fõ' lsisÿ ienE moaO;shla mdfya
    iïmQ¾Kfhkau jid ke; - kHdhd;aul bÈlsÍï fyda by< md,s; moaO;s muKs'

    Now, let's evaluate each option / oeka" wms tla tla úl,amh we.hSug ,la lruq

    Option / úl,amh 1)
    Incorrect. ATMs interact with bank systems, users, and networks. It is a highly interactive component →
    open system.
    jerÈhs' iajhxl%Sh fg,¾ hka;% nexl= moaO;s" mßYS,lhska iy cd, iuÕ wka;¾ l%shd lrhs' th b;d
    wka;¾l%shdldÍ ixrplhls → újD; moaO;shls'

    Option / úl,amh 2)
    Correct. General-purpose computers interact with users, receive input, produce output, connect to
    networks—clear characteristics of an open system.
    ksjerÈ' fmdÿ ld¾h mß.Kl mßYS,lhska iuÕ wka;¾ l%shd lrhs" wdodk ,nd .kS" m%;sodkh
    ksmojhs" cd, fj; iïnkaO fõ - újD; moaO;shl meyeÈ,s ,laIK'

    Option / úl,amh 3)
    Incorrect. The blood circulatory system is usually considered closed, as blood stays confined within
    vessels.
    jerÈhs' reêr ixirK moaO;sh idudkHfhka jid we;s nj i,lkq ,efí" ukao reêrh hd;%d ;=<
    iSud ù we;'

    Option / úl,amh 4)
    Incorrect. Mobile phones interact with networks, users, sensors, apps → they are open systems.
    jerÈhs' cx.u ÿrl:k cd," mßYS,lhska" ixfõol" fhÿï iuÕ wka;¾ l%shd lrhs → tAjd újD;
    moaO;s fõ'
RAVINDU BANDARANAYAKE                                              -42-                                      #ictfromabc



    Option / úl,amh 5)
    Incorrect. It receives sunlight (input from environment) and provides electricity → open system.
    jerÈhs' th ysre t<sh ^mßirfhka wdodkh& ,nd .kakd w;r úÿ,sh → újD; moaO;sh imhhs'

    Therefore, option 2) is the correct answer.
    tneúka" 2) úl,amh ksjerÈ ms<s;=r fõ'

 35. Which of the following graphs illustrates the Golden rule of information?
     f;dr;=re iïnkaOfhka iaj¾Kuh kS;sh (Golden rule) úoyd olajkafka my; olajd we;s l=uk
     m%ia;drfhka o?
    Value of information




                                            Value of information




                                                                                 Value of information
                                     Time                                 Time                               Time

                           1)                                      2)                                   3)
    Value of information




                                            Value of information




                                     Time                                 Time
                                4)                                 5)

    Answer: 1)

    Explanation / meyeÈ,s lsÍu

    Option/ úl,amh 1)
    This graph shows a high initial value of information that rapidly drops as time progresses. It strongly
    reflects how information is most valuable when received immediately and loses value quickly after the
    moment of need. For instance, knowing about an impending storm is only useful before it hits. This
    graph correctly represents the Golden Rule of Information.
    fuu m%ia:drh uÕska ld,h;a iuÕ f;dr;=rej, by< wdrïNl w.hla fmkajhs' th jydu ,enqKq
    úg f;dr;=re jvd;a jákd jk wdldrh iy wjYH;djh we;s jQ fudfydf;a isg blaukska jákdlu
    ke;s jk wdldrh oeä f,i ms<sìUq lrhs' ksoiqkla jYfhka" bÈßfha§ tk l=Kdgqjla .ek oek
    .ekSu m%fhdackj;a jkafka th we;sùug fmr muKs' fuu m%ia:drh f;dr;=re j, iaj¾Kuh Í;sh
    ksjerÈj ksfhdackh lrhs'

    Option/ úl,amh 2)
    Here, the value of information is low in the beginning and suddenly increases at a later time. This
    implies that information is not immediately useful, but becomes crucial much later. While this might
    apply to historical data or research findings, it does not align with the Golden Rule, which focuses
    on immediacy.
    fuys§" f;dr;=re j, jákdlu wdrïNfha § wvq jk w;r miqj yÈisfhau jeä fõ' fuhska
    weÕfjkafka f;dr;=re jydu m%fhdackj;a fkdjk kuq;a fndfyda l,lg miqj ;SrKd;aul jk njhs'
    fuh ft;sydisl o;a; fyda m¾fhaIK fidhd.ekSï i|yd wod< úh yels kuq;a" th laIKslNdjh
    flfrys wjOdkh fhduq lrk iaj¾Kuh Í;sh iuÕ iumd; fkdfõ'
RAVINDU BANDARANAYAKE                                 -43-                                           #ictfromabc


     Option/ úl,amh 3)
     This graph starts with low value, quickly rises, and then levels off, maintaining that high value. It suggests
     that information gains value early and retains it, which is partially aligned with the Golden Rule. However,
     it fails to capture the declining usefulness of information after the need passes.
     fuu m%ia:drh wvq w.hlska wdrïN ù" blaukska by< f.dia" miqj uÜgï wvq lrñka" tu by< w.h
     mj;ajd .kS' f;dr;=re l,ska w.h ,nd .kakd nj;a th r|jd .kakd nj;a" th rka Í;sh iuÕ w¾O

     j, msßfyk m%fhdackh .%yKh lr .ekSug th wiu;a fõ'

     Option/ úl,amh 4)
     This shows a gradual rise in value, indicating that information becomes more valuable over time. This
     could reflect a learning or training curve, but it contradicts the Golden Rule, which argues that delayed
     information loses its value. It may apply to data maturity in long-term analytics but is inappropriate
     for real-time relevance.
     fuh w.fha l%ufhka by< hdula fmkakqï lrhs" f;dr;=re ld,h;a iuÕ jvd;a jákd jk nj fmkakqï
     lrhs' fuh bf.kSfï fyda mqyqKq jl%hla ms<sìUq l< yels kuq;a" m%udo jQ f;dr;=re tys jákdlu ke;s
     lrk njg ;¾l lrk iaj¾Kuh Í;shg mgyeks fõ' th È.=ld,Sk úYaf,aIKj, o;a; mßK;Ndjhg

     Option/ úl,amh 5)
     This graph has a steady linear increase in value over time. It implies that information becomes more useful
     the longer it exists, which is the opposite of what the Golden Rule asserts. It may be appropriate in contexts
     like progress tracking or long-term insights, but not for time-sensitive decision making.

     1) A only.          2) B only.          3) C only.           4) A and B only.      5) B and C only.

    Explanation / meyeÈ,s lsÍu

    Statement / m%ldYh A)

    shows that each programmer can be assigned to multiple projects, and each project can have multiple
    programmers. This table enables many-to-many mappings. So, a programmer can work on more than one
    project, even at the same time. This, statement A is not always correct.
    fjku workFor(programmerId, projectId, startDate, endDate) j.=jl meje;afuka

    multiple projects via the workFor table. These multiple projects can belong to different clients. So, a

 38. Which of the following is correct?
     my; i|yka j.ka;s w;=frka ljrla ksjerÈ jkafka o?
    1) All relations are in 3rd normal form / ish¨ iïnkaO;d f;jeks m%u; wjia:dfõ mj;S'
    2) All relations except the programmer are in the 3rd normal form.
       programmer yereKq úg wfkl=;a ish¨ iïnkaO;d f;jeks m%u; wjia:dfõ mj;S'
    3) All relations except the client are in the 3rd normal form.
       client yereKq úg wfkl=;a ish¨ iïnkaO;d f;jeks m%u; wjia:dfõ mj;S'
    4) All relations except the project are in the 3rd normal form.
       project yereKq úg wfkl=;a ish¨ iïnkaO;d f;jeks m%u; wjia:dfõ mj;S'
    5) All relations except the workFor are in the 3rd normal form.
       workFor yereKq úg wfkl=;a ish¨ iïnkaO;d f;jeks m%u; wjia:dfõ mj;S'

    Answer: 2)

    Let’s analyse the given mappings / § we;s is;shïlrKhka úYaf,aIKh lruq

    programmer(programmerId, programmerName, gender, NIC, mobilePhoneNumber,
    degree, universityName)
    Primary key is programmerId. If attributes like degree depends on universityName, then there is a
    transitive dependency. So, the programmer table is not in 3NF due to potential transitive dependency.
    m%d:ñl h;=r programmerId fõ' Wmdêh jeks .=Kdx. úYaj úoHd, kduh u; r|d mj;S kï"
    ixl%dka;s mrdhla;;djhla we;' tneúka" úNj ixl%dka;s mrdhla;;djhla fya;=fjka programmer j.=j
    3NF ys fkdue;'

    client(clientId, clientName, address, telephoneNumber)
    Primary key is clientId. All other attributes are directly dependent on the key. So, this is in 3NF.
    m%d:ñl h;=r clientId fõ' wfkl=;a ish¨u .=Kdx. h;=r u; fl,skau r|d mj;S' tneúka" fuh 3NF
    ys we;'

    project(projectId, projectName, clientId, startDate, endDate, cost)
    Primary key is projectId. All attributes fully dependent on projectId. So, this is also in 3NF.
    m%d:ñl h;=r projectId fõ' ish¨u .=Kdx. projectId u; iïmQ¾Kfhkau r|d mj;S' tneúka" fuh
    3NF ys o we;'

    workFor(programmerId, projectId, startDate, endDate)
    Here, there is a composite key, programmerId and projectId. Non-key attributes, startDate and endDate,
    depend on both of the keys. So, we can assume it is in 3NF.
    fuys§" ixhqla; h;=rla we;" programmerId iy projectId. h;=re fkdjk .=Kdx." startDate iy
    endDate" h;=re folu u; r|d mj;S' tneúka" th 3NF ys we;s nj wmg Wml,amkh l< yelsh'

    From the above analysis, only programmer is not in 3rd normal form.
    by; úYaf,aIKfhka" programmer muKla 3 jk m%u; wdldrfha fkdue;'

    Therefore, the correct answer is option 2), All relations except the programmer are in the 3rd normal
    form.
    tneúka" ksjerÈ ms<s;=r úl,amh 2) fõ" programmer yer wfkl=;a ish¨u iïnkaO;d 3 jk m%u;
    wdldrfha we;'

 39. Consider the following database constraints:
     my; olajd we;s o;a; iuQod ixfrdaOl (constraints) i,ld n,kak(

    A. Primary key / m%d:ñl h;=r
    B. Data type / o;a; m%rEmh
    C. Foreign key / wd.ka;=l h;=r

    Which of the above constraint/s does/do not allow users to duplicate data in a database table?
    o;a; iuqod j.=jl" o;a; wkqmsgm;a (duplicate) lsÍug mßYS,lhkag bv fkdfokq ,nkafka by;
    fmkajd we;s ljr ixfrdaOlh$ixfrdaOl o?

    1) A only.          2) B only.          3) A and B only.   4) A and C only.       5) B and C only.
       A muKs.             B muKs.             A yd B muKs.       A yd C muKs.           B yd C muKs.
    Answer: 1)

    Let’s examine the constraints: / iïndOlhka mÍlaId lr n,uq(

    A. Primary key / m%d:ñl h;=r
    Does not allow duplicate values. Each value must be unique.
    wkqmsgm;a w.hkag bv fkdfoa' iEu w.hlau wkkH úh hq;=h'

    B. Data type / o;a; j¾.h
    Does not prevent duplication. It only ensures the kind of data (ex: integer, varchar).
    wkqmsgm;a lsÍu j<lajkafka ke;' th o;a; j¾.h muKla iy;sl lrhs ^Wod( mQ¾K ixLHd"
    varchar&'

    Only the primary key constraint prevents duplication of data in a table. So, the correct answer is A only.
    m%d:ñl h;=re iïndOlh muKla j.=jl o;a; wkqmsgm;a lsÍu j<lajhs' tneúka" ksjerÈ ms<s;=r A

 40. Which of the following actions is taken by a database management system when the SQL statement

     l%shd;aul fõ'

 41. Which of the following is correct with respect to the above tables?
     by; j.= iïnkaOfhka my; i|yka l=ula ksjerÈ jkafka o?

    1) All the tables are in third normal form / ish¨ j.= f;jeks m%u; wjia:dfõ mj;S'
    2) Normalization has been applied to these tables / fuu j.= m%u;lrKh lr we;'
    3) Integrity constraints are correctly applied to these tables.
       fuu j.= i|yd taldnoaO ixfrdaOl (integrity constraints) ksjerÈ j fhdod we;'
    4) There is no evidence to say that integrity constraints are properly applied.
       taldnoaO ixfrdaOl kshudkql+,j fhdodf.k we;s nj mejiSug lsisÿ idOlhla fuys fkdue;'
    5) Normalization and integrity constraints are properly applied.
       m%u;lrKh yd taldnoaO ixfrdaOl kshudkql+,j wdfoaY lr we;'

    Answer: 4)

    Explanation / meyeÈ,s lsÍu

    Option/ úl,amh 1)
    To be in the third normal form (3NF) the table should be in 2NF and cannot contain any transitive
    dependencies, meaning that every non-key attribute should depend only on the primary key.
    ;=kajk m%u; wdldrfhka ^3NF& ùug kï" j.=j 2NF wdldrfhka ;sìh hq;= w;r lsisÿ ixl%dka;s
    mrdhla;;djhla wvx.= úh fkdyel" tkï iEu h;=re fkdjk .=Kdx.hlau m%d:ñl h;=r u; muKla
    r|d mej;sh hq;=h'

    Here, the table decomposition suggests normalization, we cannot verify 3NF without knowing the
    functional dependencies and their primary keys. So, we cannot confirm 3NF for all tables.
    fuys§" j.= úfhdackh m%u;lrKh lsÍu fhdackd lrhs" ld¾hnoaO mrdhla;;d iy tAjdfha m%d:ñl
    h;=re fkdoek wmg 3NF i;Hdmkh l< fkdyel' tneúka" wmg ish¨u j.= i|yd 3NF ;yjqre l<
    fkdyel'

    Option/ úl,amh 2)
    Partially correct, but not the best answer. The tables are organized to reduce redundancy, suggesting some
    normalization. For example, the itemSupplier table handles a many-to-many relationship. But we cannot
    guarantee about full normalization (ex: 3NF).
    w¾O jYfhka ksjerÈ" kuq;a fyd|u ms<s;=r fkdfõ' w;sßla;;djh wvq lsÍu i|yd j.= ixúOdkh lr
    we;s w;r" hï m%u;lrKhla fhdackd lrhs' WodyrKhla f,i" itemSupplier j.=j nyq-nyq
    iïnkaO;djhla yiqrejhs' kuq;a iïmQ¾K m%u;lrKh .ek wmg iy;sl úh fkdyel ^Wod( 3NF&'

    Option/ úl,amh 3)
    Integrity constraints include primary keys (uniqueness), foreign keys (referential integrity), NOT NULL,
    etc. From the given details, we do not see any such constraints defined. Just because the data looks
    consistent does not prove constraints exist. So, we cannot claim they are correctly applied.
    wLKav;d iïndOlhkag m%d:ñl h;=re ^wkkH nj&" wd.ka;=l h;=re ^fhduq wLKav;dj&" NOT NULL
    hkdÈh we;=<;a fõ' ,nd § we;s úia;r j,ska" tjeks lsisÿ iSudjla w¾: olajd fkdue;s nj wmg
    fkdfmfka' o;a; wkql+, nj fmfkk ksid muKla iïndOlhka mj;sk nj Tmamq fkdfõ' tneúka" tAjd
    ksjerÈj fhdok nj wmg m%ldY l< fkdyel'
RAVINDU BANDARANAYAKE                               -50-                                          #ictfromabc


    Option/ úl,amh 4)
    Correct. This is the most accurate answer. No schema is provided (no PRIMARY KEY, FOREIGN KEY,
    etc.). Therefore, we cannot conclude that integrity constraints are applied, even if they seem implied by
    the data.

 46. What is the return data type of the function named " f "?

    1) Integer           2) Float            3) Boolean           4) Tuple             5) List

    Answer: 4)

    Explanation / meyeÈ,s lsÍu

    In the provided Python code / imhd we;s Python fla;fha

    def f(b):
      n1, n2 = b[0], b[0]
      for m in b:
         if m > n1:
            n1 = m
         if m < n2:
            n2 = m
      return n1, n2

    The function `f` accepts a parameter `b`, which is expected to be a list. It initializes two variables, `n1`
    and `n2`, with the first element of the list `b`. Using a `for` loop, it iterates through the list to find the
    maximum (`n1`) and minimum (`n2`) values. Finally, the function returns both values as a pair.
    f Y%s;h b mrdñ;shla ms<s.kS" th ,ehsia;=jla jkq we;ehs wfmalaId flf¾' th b ,ehsia;=fõ m<uq

    (`n2`) w.hka fidhd .ekSug th ,ehsia;=j yryd mqkrdj¾;kh fõ' wjidk jYfhka" Y%s;h hq.,hla
    f,i w.hka folu wdmiq ,nd fohs'
RAVINDU BANDARANAYAKE                                   -55-                                              #ictfromabc


    In Python, when two or more values are returned from a function, they are combined into a single tuple.
    Here, `f` returns `(n1, n2)`, which is a tuple containing the maximum and minimum values.
    Python ys" Y%s;hlska w.hka folla fyda jeä .Kkla wdmiq ,nd ÿka úg" tAjd ;ks tuple tlla njg
    tAldnoaO fõ' fuys§" f (n1, n2) wdmiq ,nd fohs" th Wmßu iy wju w.hka wvx.= tuple tlls'

 48. Consider the following Python program:
     my; fmkajd we;s mhs;ka l%uf,aLh i,lkak(
    temp = [23,45,2,–2,0]
    print(temp[::2])
    What is the output of the above program?
    by; l%uf,aLfha m%;sodkh l=ula o?

    1) [23,45]          2) [–2,0]           3) [23,2,0]        4) [2,–2,0]         5) [23,45,2,–2,0]

    Answer: 3)

    Explanation / meyeÈ,s lsÍu

    The provided Python program uses list slicing to extract specific elements from the list temp. The list temp
    is defined as [23, 45, 2, -2, 0]. The slicing operation temp[::2] starts from the beginning of the list and
    selects every second element by skipping one element in between. In this case, the elements at indices 0,
    2 and 4 are selected, which correspond to the values 23, 2 and 0, respectively. As a result, the output of
    the program is the list [23, 2, 0].
    imhd we;s mhs;ka jevigyk temp ,ehsia;=fjka ksYaÑ; uQ,dx. Wmqgd .ekSu i|yd ,ehsia;= slicing
    Ndú;d lrhs' ,ehsia;= temp [23, 45, 2, -2, 0] f,i w¾: olajd we;' temp[::2] slicing fufyhqu ,ehsia;=fõ
    wdrïNfha isg wdrïN jk w;r tla uQ,dx.hla uÕ yeÍfuka iEu fojk uQ,dx.hlau f;dard .kS' fuu
    wjia:dfõ§" 0, 2 iy 4 o¾Ylj, uQ,dx. f;dard .kq ,efí" tAjd ms<sfj,ska 23, 2 iy 0 w.hkag
    wkqrEm fõ' tys m%;sM,hla f,i" jevigyfka m%;sodkh [23, 2, 0] ,ehsia;=j fõ'

    This behavior is consistent in both Python 2 and Python 3, as the slicing syntax and functionality remain
    the same across these versions. The slicing operation produces a new list that contains the selected
    elements, and the output [23, 2, 0] will be identical regardless of whether the program is run in Python 2
    or Python 3.
    fuu wkqjdo yryd slicing lsÍfï jdlH LKavh iy l%shdldÍ;ajh tf,iu mj;sk neúka" Python 2 iy
    Python 3 hk foflysu fuu yeisÍu wkql+, fõ' slicing fufyhqu f;dard.;a uQ,dx. wvx.= kj
    ,ehsia;=jla ksmojk w;r" jevigyk Python 2 fyda Python 3 ys l%shd;aul fõo hkak wod< fkdfõ
    [23, 2, 0] m%;sodkh iudk jkq we;'

       Step        Index      Value      Included in Result?
      Step 1         0         23               Yes
      Step 2         1         45                No
      Step 3         2          2               Yes
      Step 4         3         -2                No
      Step 5         4          0               Yes

    Therefore, option 3) is the correct answer.
    tneúka" 3) úl,amh ksjerÈ ms<s;=r fõ'
